Two Constraint Compilation Methods for Lifted Planning
Periklis Mantenoglou, Luigi Bonassi, Enrico Scala, Pedro Zuidberg Dos Martires

Abstract
We study planning in a fragment of PDDL with qualitative state-trajectory constraints, capturing safety requirements, task ordering conditions, and intermediate sub-goals commonly found in real-world problems. A prominent approach to tackle such problems is to compile their constraints away, leading to a problem that is supported by state-of-the-art planners. Unfortunately, existing compilers do not scale on problems with a large number of objects and high-arity actions, as they necessitate grounding the problem before compilation. To address this issue, we propose two methods for compiling away constraints without grounding, making them suitable for large-scale planning problems. We prove the correctness of our compilers and outline their worst-case time complexity. Moreover, we present a reproducible empirical evaluation on the domains used in the latest International Planning Competition. Our results demonstrate that our methods are efficient and produce planning specifications that are orders of magnitude more succinct than the ones produced by compilers that ground the domain, while remaining competitive when used for planning with a state-of-the-art planner.



1
Introduction

Planning involves identifying a sequence of actions that brings about a desired goal state, and is vital in several real-world tasks
(
31
;
32
)
.
In addition to final goals, a planning task may require the satisfaction of certain temporal constraints throughout the execution of the plan
(
1
)
. These constraints may enforce, e.g., a set of conditions that must be avoided
(
34
)
, or the ordered resolution of certain tasks
(
15
)
, both of which are common in planning problems
(
21
)
. Many planning approaches supporting these types of specifications have been considered in the literature
(
1
;
25
;
24
;
22
;
5
)
.

In this paper, we focus on the fragment of PDDL that introduced and standardised constraints over trajectories of states
(
13
)
. Some approaches handle these types of constraints directly in the search engine
(
3
;
20
;
11
)
, while others remove the constraints using compilation approaches
(
26
;
6
)
. Alternatively, these constraints can be translated into Linear Temporal Logic (LTL)
(
27
)
, or one of its variants
(
10
)
, and handled by existing compilers
(
2
;
36
;
7
)
. The compilation step yields an equivalent constraint-free problem that can be solved by off-the-shelf planners
(
14
;
33
)
.

For PDDL qualitative state-trajectory constraints, TCORE
(
4
)
constitutes the state-of-the-art compilation approach.
Specifically, TCORE employs a so-called regression operator
(
29
)
that expresses the circumstances under which an action may affect constraint satisfaction.
However, TCORE’s regression operator only works on ground actions, requiring compilation over the fully grounded problem, which can be orders of magnitude larger than the original (non-ground) problem.

Typically, the cost of grounding increases with the number of objects.
This becomes a critical issue in domains with high-arity actions, which have to be grounded once for each valid combination of objects in their arguments. Consider, e.g., the following variant of the
Blocksworld
domain.

Example 1

(
Blocksworld2
)
.

Blocksworld
involves rearranging stacks of blocks on a table to reach a final configuration by moving one block at a time.
States are described by atoms such as
\it\mathtt{clear}(b)
, expressing that there is no block on top of block
\it b
, and
\it\mathtt{on}(b_{1},b_{2})
, expressing that block
\it b_{1}
is on top of block
\it b_{2}
.
Blocksworld2
extends
Blocksworld
by allowing operations on towers consisting of two blocks. Action
\it\mathtt{pickup2}
, e.g., allows the agent to pick up a two-block tower.
Grounding a
Blocksworld2
problem introduces one ground instance of the
\it\mathtt{pickup2}
action for each (ordered) pair of blocks in the problem.
\it\lozenge

Although large instances of such domains can be handled by lifted planners
(
9
;
19
;
17
;
18
;
12
;
8
)
, these approaches do not consider constraints. In
Blocksworld2
, these constraints might be, for example, that block
\it b_{1}
must never be on the table, or that placing
\it b_{1}
on top of
\it b_{2}
is only allowed after placing
\it b_{3}
on the table.

To address this issue, we propose two constraint compilers that are
lifted
, in the sense that they avoid unnecessary grounding.
We summarise our
contributions
as follows.

First
, we propose LiftedTCORE, a constraint compiler that employs a lifted variant of the regression operator used by TCORE
in order to bypass grounding.

Second
, we propose the Lifted Constraint Compiler (LCC) that compiles away constraints without grounding and without the use of a regression operator.

Third
, we demonstrate the correctness of LiftedTCORE and LCC, and outline their worst-case time complexity.

Fourth
, we present an empirical evaluation on challenging planning domains from the latest International Planning Competition
(
35
)
.
Our results demonstrate that LiftedTCORE and LCC are efficient and lead to compiled specifications that are significantly more succinct than the ones produced by compilers that ground the domain, while remaining competitive when used for planning with a state-of-the-art planner.

The proofs of all propositions are provided in Appendices
A
and
B
.
Appendix
C
contains comparative discussion on constraint compilers and regression variants found in the literature, while Appendices
D
and
E
provide details on the benchmark we employed and additional experimental results, respectively.

2
Background

2.1
Planning with Constraints in PDDL

A planning problem is a tuple
\it\Pi\val\langle F,O,A,I,G,C\rangle
, where
\it F
is a set of atoms,
\it O
is a set of objects,
\it A
is a set of actions,
\it I\subseteq F
is an initial state,
\it G
is a formula over
\it F
denoting the goal of the problem, and
\it C
is a set of constraints.
Each action
\it a\in A
comprises a list of arguments
\it Arg(a)
, a precondition
\it Pre(a)
, which is a formula over
\it F
, and a set of conditional effects
\it Eff(a)
.
Each conditional effect in
\it Eff(a)
is an expression
\it\forall\vec{z}{:}\ c\rhd e
, where
\it c
is a formula,
\it e
is a literal—both constructed based on the atoms in
\it F
—and
\it\vec{z}
is a set of variables.

\it\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e
expresses that, for each possible substitution
\it\theta_{z}
of the variables in
\it\vec{z}
with objects from
\it O
, if the condition
\it c|_{\theta_{z}}
is true, then effect
\it e|_{\theta_{z}}
is brought about
(
23
)
, where
\it x|_{\theta}
denotes the application of substitution
\it\theta
in formula
\it x
.
We use
\it Eff^{+}(a)
(resp.
\it Eff^{-}(a)
) to denote the set of effects of an action
\it a
where literal
\it e
is positive (negative), and
\it a^{\mathbin{\text{\hbox to4.6pt{\vbox to4.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.30002pt\lower-2.03357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

.

We focus on a fragment of PDDL that includes
\it\mathsf{Always}
,
\it\mathsf{Sometime}
,
\it\mathsf{AtMostOnce}
,
\it\mathsf{SometimeBefore}
and
\it\mathsf{SometimeAfter}
constraints
(
13
)
.
Given a sequence of states
\it\sigma\val\langle s_{0},\dots,s_{n}\rangle
and first-order formulae
\it\phi
and
\it\psi
, these constraint types are defined as follows:

•

\it\sigma\models\mathsf{Always}(\phi)
(or
\it\mathsf{A}(\phi)
) iff
\it\forall i{:}\ 0\leq i\leq n,s_{i}\models\phi
, i.e.,
\it\phi
holds in every state of
\it\sigma
.

•

\it\sigma\models\mathsf{Sometime}(\phi)
(
\it\mathsf{ST}(\phi)
) iff
\it\exists i{:}\ 0\leq i\leq n,s_{i}\models\phi
, i.e.,
\it\phi
holds in at least one state of
\it\sigma
.

•

\it\sigma\models\mathsf{AtMostOnce}(\phi)
(
\it\mathsf{AO}(\phi)
) iff
\it\forall i{:}\ 0\leq i\leq n
, if
\it s_{i}\models\phi
, then
\it\exists j{:}\ j\geq i
such that
\it\forall k{:}\ i\leq k\leq j,s_{k}\models\phi
and
\it\forall k{:}\ j<k\leq n,s_{k}\models\neg\phi
, i.e.,
\it\phi
is true in at most one continuous subsequence of
\it\sigma
.

•

\it\sigma\models\mathsf{SometimeBefore}(\phi,\psi)
(
\it\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)
) iff
\it\forall i{:}\ 0\leq i\leq n
, if
\it s_{i}\models\phi
then
\it\exists j{:}\ 0\leq j<i,s_{j}\models\psi
, i.e., if there is a state
\it s_{i}
where
\it\phi
holds, then there is a state that is before
\it s_{i}
in
\it\sigma
where
\it\psi
holds.

•

\it\sigma\models\mathsf{SometimeAfter}(\phi,\psi)
(
\it\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)
) iff
\it\forall i{:}\ 0\leq i\leq n
, if
\it s_{i}\models\phi
then
\it\exists j{:}\ i\leq j\leq n,s_{j}\models\psi
, i.e., if there is a state
\it s_{i}
where
\it\phi
holds, then
\it\psi
also holds at state
\it s_{i}
or there is a state that is after
\it s_{i}
in
\it\sigma
where
\it\psi
holds.

A plan
\it\pi
for problem
\it\Pi\val\langle F,O,A,I,G,C\rangle
is a sequence of ground actions
\it\langle a^{\mathbin{\text{\hbox to4.6pt{\vbox to4.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.30002pt\lower-2.03357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.34 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}
{}
{}
{}
{}
{}
{}
{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.91 0.37 L 2.91 0.37 M -1.94 -1.08 L 1.94 -1.08 M -0.97 -2.54 L 0.97 -2.54}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

2.2
Regression

The regression of a formula
\it\phi
through an action
\it a
is the weakest condition that has to hold in order to guarantee the satisfaction of
\it\phi
after the application of
\it a
.

29

\it\phi
and

\it\phi
appearing in a constraint and each action
\it a^{\mathbin{\text{\hbox to4.6pt{\vbox to4.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.30002pt\lower-2.03357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.34 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}
{}
{}
{}
{}
{}
{}
{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.91 0.37 L 2.91 0.37 M -1.94 -1.08 L 1.94 -1.08 M -0.97 -2.54 L 0.97 -2.54}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-0.4}{0.3}{0.0}{0.0pt}{-0.7836pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{}}
.
Subsequently, TCORE introduces
\it R(\phi,a^{\mathbin{\text{\hbox to4.6pt{\vbox to4.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.30002pt\lower-2.03357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.34 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}
{}
{}
{}
{}
{}
{}
{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.91 0.37 L 2.91 0.37 M -1.94 -1.08 L 1.94 -1.08 M -0.97 -2.54 L 0.97 -2.54}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-0.4}{0.3}{0.0}{0.0pt}{-0.7836pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{}})
formulae and ‘monitoring atoms’—whose purpose is to track the status of constraints—in action preconditions and effects, capturing the semantics of the constraints in the compiled problem.

3
LiftedTCORE

3.1
Lifted Regression

The idea behind constructing LiftedTCORE is to generalize the regression operator from the ground domain to the lifted domain.
Consequently, LiftedTCORE will need to compute the lifted regression operator
\it R^{L}(\phi,a)
for each action
\it a
and each formula
\it\phi
appearing in an argument of a constraint.
We achieve this by constructing a lifted gamma operator
\it\Gamma^{L}_{l}(a)
and adapting Definition
1
accordingly.

Similar to
\it\Gamma_{l}(a)
,
\it\Gamma^{L}_{l}(a)
expresses the weakest condition that leads to the satisfaction of
\it l
after applying action
\it a
, with the difference of
\it\Gamma^{L}_{l}(a)
operating on non-ground
\it l
and
\it a
.

We start with the case of a formula
\it\phi
whose truth value may be affected only by
\it c\triangleright e
effects of action
\it a
, i.e., the only set to consider in
\it\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e
is the empty set (
\it\vec{z}=\emptyset
).
For each literal
\it l
in
\it\phi
and each effect
\it c\triangleright e
of
\it a
, we compute the most general unifier
\it\xi(l,e)
between
\it l
and
\it e
(if any) and derive the weakest condition
\it w^{c}_{l}(c\triangleright e)
under which an action with effect
\it c\triangleright e
may bring about
\it l
.
We derive
\it\xi(l,e)
via
30
’s resolution algorithm
(
30
)
by using the most general resolution, in the sense that variables are grounded to constants only when required.

Definition 2

(Weakest Condition
\it w^{c}_{l}(c\triangleright e)
)
.

The weakest condition
\it w^{c}_{l}(c\triangleright e)
under which effect
\it c\triangleright e
brings about literal
\it l
is:

\it\displaystyle w^{c}_{l}(c\triangleright e)\val\begin{cases}c\wedge\bigwedge_{\begin{subarray}{c}(t_{i}\doteq u_{i})\in\xi(l,e)\end{subarray}}(t_{i}=u_{i})&\text{if }\exists\xi(l,e)\\
\bot&\text{if }\nexists\xi(l,e)\end{cases}

where
\it t_{i}
and
\it u_{i}
denote the arguments of the literals
\it l
and
\it e
respectively, and
\it t_{i}\doteq u_{i}
denotes their unification.

\it\blacksquare

Example 2

(Weakest Condition
\it w^{c}_{l}(c\triangleright e)
)
.

Consider the
Blocksworld2
domain, and action
\it\mathtt{putdown2}
, which allows the agent to place a block or a two-block tower on the table.

\it\mathtt{putdown2}
has one argument
\it b
and its effects are:

•

\it\mathtt{handEmpty}
, i.e., the agent’s hand is empty,

•

\it\mathtt{onTable}(b)
, i.e., block
\it b
is on the table,

•

\it\neg\mathtt{holding}(b)
, i.e., the agent is not holding
\it b
,

•

\it\neg\mathtt{towerBase}(b)\triangleright\mathtt{clear}(b)
, i.e., if
\it b
is not the base of a block tower, then
\it b
is clear.

Consider literals
\it\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1})
and
\it\mathtt{clear}(b_{5})
, where
\it b_{1}
and
\it b_{5}
are ground.

\it\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1})
can only be unified with effect
\it\mathtt{onTable}(b)
of
\it\mathtt{putdown2}
via
\it b\doteq b_{1}
, while
\it\mathtt{clear}(b_{5})
can only be unified with literal
\it\mathtt{clear}(b)
of effect
\it\neg\mathtt{towerBase}(b)\triangleright\mathtt{clear}(b)
via
\it b\doteq b_{5}
.
Thus, we have:

\it\displaystyle w^{c}_{\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1})}(\mathtt{onTable}(b))\val(b\val b_{1})

\it\displaystyle w^{c}_{\mathtt{clear}(b_{5})}(\neg\mathtt{towerBase}(b)\triangleright\mathtt{clear}(b))\val

\it\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt\neg\mathtt{towerBase}(b)\wedge(b\val b_{5})

\it\lozenge

Next, we extend Definition
2
to handle
\it\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e
effects for a non-empty set
\it\vec{z}
.
In the case of such an effect, a variable
\it u
appearing in an argument of
\it e
may be: (i) a parameter of
\it a
, or (ii) a variable in
\it\vec{z}
.
Definition
2
handles case (i) by introducing equalities between the action parameters in
\it e
and the corresponding arguments of
\it l
.
Case (ii), however, needs a different type of treatment as the scope of a variable
\it u\in\vec{z}
is only within
\it\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e
, and thus, if we were to introduce an equality between
\it u
and an argument of
\it l
in
\it R^{L}_{l}(a)
, then
\it u
would be a free variable in
\it R^{L}_{l}(a)
.

To tackle this issue, we leverage the semantics of
\it\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e
, according to which
\it\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e
is equivalent to introducing one effect
\it c|_{\theta_{z}}\triangleright e|_{\theta_{z}}
for each possible substitution
\it\theta_{z}
of the variables in
\it\vec{z}
with domain objects
(
23
)
. As a result,
\it e
can be unified with
\it l
iff there is a substitution
\it\theta_{z}(l,e)
such that
\it e|_{\theta_{z}(l,e)}
can be unified with
\it l
.
If there is such a substitution, then, in order for effect
\it\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e
to bring about
\it l
, there needs to be an assignment to the variables of
\it\vec{z}
that appear in
\it c
but not in
\it e
, i.e., the variables in set
\it\vec{z}^{f}
, such that
\it c|_{\theta_{z}(l,e)}
becomes true.
In other words, formula
\it\exists\vec{z}^{f}\ c|_{\theta_{z}(l,e)}
needs to hold.
Then, the weakest condition
\it w_{l}(\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e)
under which
\it\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e
brings about
\it l
requires for
\it\exists\vec{z}^{f}\ c|_{\theta_{z}(l,e)}
to hold under a variable assignment that unifies
\it l
and
\it e|_{\theta_{z}(l,e)}
.

Definition 3

(
\it z
-substitution)
.

Consider a literal
\it l
and a conditional effect
\it\forall\vec{z}{:}\ c\rhd e
, such that
\it l
and
\it e
can be unified and
\it\xi(l,e)
is their most general unifier.
We define the
\it z
-substitution
\it\theta_{z}(l,e)
of
\it l
and
\it e
as:

\it\displaystyle\theta_{z}(l,e)\val\{u_{i}\mapsto t_{i}\ |\ (t_{i}\doteq u_{i})\in\xi(l,e)\wedge u_{i}\in\vec{z}\}

where
\it x\mapsto y
denotes that term
\it y
substitutes term
\it x
.

\it\blacksquare

Definition 4

(Weakest Condition
\it w_{l}(\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e)
)
.

The weakest condition under which
\it\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e
brings about
\it l
is:

\it\displaystyle w_{l}(\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e)\val\begin{cases}\exists\vec{z}^{f}\ c|_{\theta_{z}(l,e)}\wedge\bigwedge\limits_{\mathclap{(t_{i}\doteq u_{i})\in\xi(l,e)\wedge u_{i}\notin\vec{z}}}(t_{i}=u_{i})&\text{if }\exists\xi(l,e)\\
\bot&\text{if }\nexists\xi(l,e)\end{cases}

where
\it\vec{z}^{f}\val\{z\in\vec{z}\ |\ \nexists t_{i}:(z\mapsto t_{i})\in\theta_{z}(l,e)\}
.

\it\blacksquare

According to Definition
4
, when
\it\vec{z}
is empty,
\it w_{l}(\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e)
coincides with
\it w^{c}_{l}(c\triangleright e)
.

Example 3

(Weakest Condition
\it w_{l}(\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e)
)
.

Consider that literal
\it l
is
\it\mathtt{clear}(b_{5})
and suppose that action
\it\mathtt{putdown2}
additionally has the following effect:

•

\it\forall\{topb\}{:}\ \mathtt{on}(topb,b)\triangleright\mathtt{clear}(topb)
, i.e., any block
\it topb
that is on top of block
\it b
is clear.

\it\mathtt{clear}(b_{5})
unifies with
\it\mathtt{clear}(topb)
via
\it{topb\doteq b_{5}}
, and, since
\it topb
is a
\it\vec{z}
variable of the effect, we have
\it\theta_{z}(\mathtt{clear}(b_{5}),\mathtt{clear}(topb))\val\{topb\mapsto b_{5}\}
, while
\it\vec{z}^{f}
is empty.
Therefore, we have:

\it\displaystyle w_{l}(\forall\{topb\}{:}\ \mathtt{on}(topb,b)\triangleright\mathtt{clear}(topb))\val\mathtt{on}(b_{5},b)

\it\lozenge

Definition 5

(Lifted Gamma Operator
\it\Gamma^{L}_{l}(a)
)
.

Given an action
\it a
and a literal
\it l
, the lifted gamma operator is defined as:
\it\Gamma^{L}_{l}(a)\val\bigvee_{\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e\in Eff(a)}w_{l}(\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e)
.

\it\blacksquare

According to Definition
5
,
\it\Gamma^{L}_{l}(a)
is the weakest condition under which action
\it a
brings about
\it l
via one of its effects.

Example 4

(Lifted Gamma Operator
\it\Gamma^{L}_{l}(a)
)
.

Based on the results in Examples
2
and
3
, We have:

\it\displaystyle\Gamma^{L}_{\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1})}(\mathtt{putdown2})\val

\it\displaystyle(b\val b_{1})

\it\displaystyle\Gamma^{L}_{\mathtt{clear}(b_{5})}(\mathtt{putdown2})\val

\it\displaystyle(\neg\mathtt{towerBase}(b)\wedge(b\val b_{5}))

\it\displaystyle\vee\mathtt{on}(b_{5},b)

\it\lozenge

Definition 6

(Lifted Regression Operator
\it R^{L}(\phi,a)
)
.

Consider a first-order formula
\it\phi
and an action
\it a
.
The lifted regression
\it R^{L}(\phi,a)
is the formula obtained from
\it\phi
by replacing every atom
\it f
in
\it\phi
with
\it\Gamma^{L}_{f}(a)\vee(f\wedge\neg\Gamma^{L}_{\neg f}(a))
.

\it\blacksquare

According to Definition
6
,
\it R^{L}(\phi,a)
is the weakest condition under which
\it\phi
is true after the execution of action
\it a
.

Example 5

(Lifted Regression
\it R^{L}(\phi,a)
)
.

Based on the results in Example
4
, and since
\it\Gamma^{L}_{\neg\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1})}(\mathtt{putdown2})\val\bot
and
\it\Gamma^{L}_{\neg\mathtt{clear}(b_{5})}(\mathtt{putdown2})\val\bot
, we have:

\it\displaystyle R^{L}(\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1}),\mathtt{putdown2})\val(b\val b_{1})\vee\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1})

\it\displaystyle R^{L}(\mathtt{clear}(b_{5}),\mathtt{putdown2})\val\mathtt{on}(b_{5},b)\vee\mathtt{clear}(b_{5})\vee

\it\displaystyle\hskip 100.0pt(\neg\mathtt{towerBase}(b)\wedge(b\val b_{5}))

\it\lozenge

3.2
The LiftedTCORE Compiler

LiftedTCORE follows the same steps as the TCORE compiler
(
4
)
, with the exception of using lifted regression.
As in TCORE, the main intuition behind LiftedTCORE is to foresee that an action
\it a
will affect the truth value of a formula
\it\phi
appearing in a constraint by checking whether
\it R^{L}(\phi,a)
holds.
If it does hold, then LiftedTCORE sets a so-called ‘monitoring atom’, in order to express the status of
\it\phi
after an execution of
\it a
.
Contrary to TCORE, LiftedTCORE introduces the necessary regression formulae and monitoring atoms without grounding the problem, thus avoiding the combinatorial explosion induced by grounding.

Algorithm
1
outlines the steps of LiftedTCORE.
First, we check if an
\it\mathsf{A}
or
\it\mathsf{SB}
constraint (c.f. Section
2.1
) is violated in the initial state (line
3
).
Since their violation is irrevocable, we deem the problem unsolvable (line
4
).
Next, we introduce the monitoring atoms required to track the constraints of the problem (line
5
).

\it hold_{c}
expresses that constraint
\it c
is satisfied based on the state trajectory induced so far.

\it seen_{\phi}
expresses that there is a state in the induced trajectory where
\it\phi
holds.

\it hold_{c}
atoms are used to monitor
\it\mathsf{ST}
and
\it\mathsf{SA}
constraints, while
\it seen_{\phi}
atoms are required for
\it\mathsf{SB}
and
\it\mathsf{AO}
constraints.
Constraints of type
\it\mathsf{A}
do not demand monitoring atoms.
We augment the initial state
\it I
with the monitoring atoms that are satisfied in
\it I
(line
6
).

Afterwards, for each action
\it a
, we compute the preconditions and the effects that need to be added in
\it a
in order to capture the semantics of the constraints within the compiled problem (lines
8
–
11
).
For a
\it\mathsf{A}(\phi)
constraint, we need to add precondition
\it R^{L}(\phi,a)
in
\it a
, stating that
\it\phi
needs to be true after the execution of
\it a
(line
16
).
In the case of
\it\mathsf{ST}(\phi)
, we activate monitoring atom
\it hold_{\hskip 1.0pt\mathsf{ST}(\phi)}
if
\it\phi
is true after executing
\it a
(line
18
).
For
\it\mathsf{AO}(\phi)
, we set
\it seen_{\phi}
when
\it a
brings about
\it\phi
(line
20
) and forbid the execution of
\it a
when
\it seen_{\phi}
holds,
\it\phi
is false and
\it a
would bring about
\it\phi
(line
21
), thus prohibiting
\it\phi
from occurring more than once.
For
\it\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)
, we set
\it seen_{\psi}
when
\it a
brings about
\it\psi
, and forbid the execution of
\it a
when it would bring about
\it\phi
while
\it seen_{\phi}
does not hold.
For
\it\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)
, we set
\it hold_{\hskip 1.0pt\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)}
if
\it a
brings about
\it\psi
, and
\it\neg hold_{\hskip 1.0pt\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)}
if
\it a
brings about a state where
\it\phi\wedge\neg\psi
holds.
Lastly, we require that all
\it hold_{c}
monitoring atoms need to be true in a goal state, reflecting that all
\it\mathsf{ST}
and
\it\mathsf{SA}
must have been satisfied by the end of the plan (line
12
).

Algorithm 1

LiftedTCORE

1:

Planning problem
\it\Pi=\langle F,O,A,I,G,\mathcal{C}\rangle
.

2:

Planning problem
\it\Pi^{\prime}=\langle F^{\prime},O,A^{\prime},I^{\prime},G^{\prime},\emptyset\rangle

3:

if

\it\exists\mathsf{A}(\phi)\in\mathcal{C}:I\models\neg\phi\vee\exists\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{C}:I\models\phi

then

4:

return
Unsolvable Problem

5:

\it F^{\prime}\leftarrow F\cup\ \bigcup\limits_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}c:\mathsf{ST}(\phi)\in\mathcal{C}\ \vee\\
c:\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}}\{hold_{c}\}\;\cup\ \bigcup\limits_{\mathclap{\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{C}}}\{seen_{\psi}\}\cup\ \bigcup\limits_{\mathclap{\mathsf{AO}(\phi)\in\mathcal{C}}}\{seen_{\phi}\}

6:

\it I^{\prime}{\leftarrow}I\cup\ \bigcup\limits_{\mathclap{c:\mathsf{ST}(\phi)\in\mathcal{C}}}\{hold_{c}\ |\ I\models\phi\}\cup\ \bigcup\limits_{\mathclap{c:\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{C}}}\{hold_{c}\ |\ I\models\psi{\vee}\neg\phi\}

7:

\it\cup\ \bigcup\limits_{\mathclap{\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{C}}}\{seen_{\psi}\ |\ I\models\psi\}\cup\ \bigcup\limits_{\mathclap{\mathsf{AO}(\phi)\in\mathcal{C}}}\{seen_{\phi}\ |\ I\models\phi\}

8:

for all

\it a\in A

do

9:

\it P,E\leftarrow\textsc{CompileCRegression}(a,\mathcal{C})

10:

\it Pre(a)\leftarrow Pre(a)\wedge\bigwedge_{p\in P}p

11:

\it Eff(a)\leftarrow Eff(a)\cup E

12:

return

\it\langle F^{\prime},O,A,I^{\prime},G\wedge\bigwedge_{hold_{c}\in F^{\prime}}hold_{c},\emptyset\rangle

13:

function

CompileCRegression
(
\it a,\mathcal{C}
)

14:

\it P,E=\{\},\{\}

15:

for

\it c\in\mathcal{C}

do

16:

if

\it c
is
\it\mathsf{A}(\phi)

then

\it P\leftarrow P\cup\{R^{L}(\phi,a)\}

17:

else

if

\it c
is
\it\mathsf{ST}(\phi)

then

18:

\it E\leftarrow E\cup\{R^{L}(\phi,a)\triangleright\{hold_{c}\}\}

19:

else

if

\it c
is
\it\mathsf{AO}(\phi)

then

20:

\it E\leftarrow E\cup\{R^{L}(\phi,a)\triangleright\{seen_{\phi}\}\}

21:

\it P\leftarrow P\cup\{\neg(seen_{\phi}\wedge\neg\phi\wedge R^{L}(\phi,a))\}

22:

else

if

\it c
is
\it\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)

then

23:

\it E\leftarrow E\cup\{R^{L}(\psi,a)\triangleright\{seen_{\psi}\}\}

24:

\it P\leftarrow P\cup\{R^{L}(\phi,a)\rightarrow seen_{\psi}\}

25:

else

if

\it c
is
\it\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)

then

26:

\it E\leftarrow E\cup\{R^{L}(\psi,a)\triangleright\{hold_{c}\}\}

27:

\it\cup\{{R^{L}(\phi,a){\wedge}\neg R^{L}(\psi,a){\rhd}\{\neg hold_{c}\}}\}

28:

return

\it P
,
\it E

Example 6

(LiftedTCORE)
.

Consider a
Blocksworld2
problem with the following constraints:
\it\mathsf{ST}(\mathtt{clear}(b_{5}))
,
\it\mathsf{AO}(\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1}))
,
\it\mathsf{SB}(\mathtt{clear}(b_{5}),\exists topb:\mathtt{on}(topb,b_{3}))
.
To compile away these constraints, LiftedTCORE introduces monitoring atoms
\it hold_{\hskip 1.0pt\mathsf{ST}(\mathtt{clear}(b_{5}))}
,
\it seen_{\psi}
and
\it seen_{\phi}
, where
\it\psi
is
\it\exists topb:\mathtt{on}(topb,b_{3})
and
\it\phi
is
\it\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1})
, and updates the initial state with these atoms according to line
6
.
Subsequently, LiftedTCORE introduces a set of action-specific preconditions and effects.
For action
\it\mathtt{putdown2}
, e.g., LiftedTCORE adds preconditions
\it P
and effects
\it E
, i.e.:

\it\displaystyle P\val\{

\it\displaystyle R^{L}(\mathtt{clear}(b_{5}),\mathtt{putdown2})\rightarrow seen_{\psi},

\it\displaystyle\neg(seen_{\phi}\wedge\neg\phi\wedge R^{L}(\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1}),\mathtt{putdown2})\}

\it\displaystyle E\val\{

\it\displaystyle R^{L}(\mathtt{clear}(b_{5}),\mathtt{putdown2})\triangleright hold_{\hskip 1.0pt\mathsf{ST}(\mathtt{clear}(b_{5}))},

\it\displaystyle R^{L}(\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1}),\mathtt{putdown2})\triangleright seen_{\phi}\}

where expressions
\it R^{L}(\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1}),\mathtt{putdown2})
and
\it R^{L}(\mathtt{clear}(b_{5}),\mathtt{putdown2})
were derived in Example
5
.

\it\lozenge

According to line
23
of Algorithm
1
, set
\it E
of Example
6
should have included effect
\it R^{L}(\psi,\mathtt{putdown2})\triangleright seen_{\psi}
.
The reason for its omission was based on the fact that
\it R^{L}(\psi,\mathtt{putdown2})\val\psi
, which implies that
\it\psi
holds after the execution of action
\it\mathtt{putdown2}
in a state
\it s
iff
\it\psi
holds at
\it s
.
Therefore, if
\it R^{L}(\psi,\mathtt{putdown2})\psi
holds, then there is an earlier action
\it a
, where
\it R^{L}(\psi,a)\nval\psi
, that made
\it\psi
true, and thus set atom
\it seen_{\psi}
.
Thus, it would be redundant to reinstate
\it seen_{\psi}
when
\it\mathtt{putdown2}
is executed.
By omitting regression formulae such that
\it R^{L}(\phi,a)\val\phi
, LiftedTCORE compresses the compiled specifications without sacrificing correctness.

3.3
Theoretical Properties.

We prove that LiftedTCORE is correct, i.e., a problem has the same solutions as its compiled version produced by LiftedTCORE, and outline its complexity.
Towards correctness, we first demonstrate a property of lifted regression.

\it\blacklozenge

Proposition 1

(Correctness of LiftedTCORE)
.

If LiftedTCORE compiles a problem
\it\Pi
into problem
\it\Pi^{\prime}
, then a plan
\it\pi
is valid for
\it\Pi
iff
\it\pi
is valid for
\it\Pi^{\prime}
.

\it\blacklozenge

Proposition 2

(Complexity of LiftedTCORE)
.

Assuming that the nesting depth of quantifiers in constraint formulae is bounded by constant
\it b
,
the worst-case time complexity of compiling a problem
\it\Pi
with LiftedTCORE is
\it\mathcal{O}(n_{c}n_{f}^{b}{+}n_{a}n_{c}n^{2}_{f}n_{k})
, where
\it n_{c}
,
\it n_{f}
,
\it n_{a}
and
\it n_{k}
denote, respectively, the number of constraints, the number of atoms, the number of actions and the maximum atom arity in
\it\Pi
.

\it\blacklozenge

Allowing an unbounded quantifier nesting in constraint formulae makes the step of checking whether the initial state models such a formula (line
3
) PSPACE-complete
(
37
)
, dominating the complexity of LiftedTCORE.
In practice, however, the quantifier nesting depth is shallow.

4
The Lifted Constraint Compiler

Next, we discuss the ‘Lifted Constraint Compiler’ (LCC).
LCC does not use lifted regression, and works by computing a set of preconditions
\it P
and a set of effects
\it E
that are
independent
from actions.
Effects
\it E
record the status of constraints: when an action
\it a
is executed in a state
\it s
,
\it E
will introduce in the next state
\it s^{\prime}
information regarding the status of the constraints in
\it s
.
The purpose of preconditions
\it P
is to prevent the execution of further actions in a state
\it s
when a constraint has been violated.
These new preconditions and effects are shared among all actions.
In this way, LCC monitors constraint violation without the need for regression, i.e., instead of foreseeing that an action
\it a
will affect a formula
\it\phi
of a constraint via the regression of
\it\phi
through
\it a
, LCC allows the application of any executable action
\it a
but then blocks subsequent state expansion if
\it a
led to constraint violation.
Following this schema, when a state
\it s
where the goal is satisfied is reached, there has not been an earlier check on whether the constraints are satisfied in
\it s
.
To address this, LCC introduces a new action
\it fin
whose purpose is to verify that a state of the plan that satisfies the goal also satisfies the constraints.
To enforce that
\it fin
is the last action executed, we use a new atom
\it end
that marks the end of the plan.

Algorithm 2

LCC

1:

Planning Problem
\it\Pi\val\langle F,O,A,I,G,C\rangle
.

2:

Planning Problem
\it\Pi\val\langle F^{\prime},O,A^{\prime},I^{\prime},G^{\prime},\emptyset\rangle
.

3:

\it F^{\prime}\leftarrow F\cup\ \bigcup\limits_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}c:\mathsf{ST}(\phi)\in\mathcal{C}\ \vee\\
c:\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}}\{hold_{c}\}\cup\bigcup\limits_{\mathclap{\mathsf{AO}(\phi)\in\mathcal{C}}}\{seen_{\phi},prevent_{\phi}\}\cup

\it\bigcup\limits_{\mathclap{\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{C}}}\{seen_{\psi}\}\cup\{end\}

4:

\it P,E\leftarrow\textsc{CompileC}(\mathcal{C})

5:

for all

\it a\in A

do

6:

\it Pre(a)\leftarrow Pre(a)\wedge\bigwedge_{p\in P}p\wedge\neg end

7:

\it Eff(a)\leftarrow Eff(a)\cup E

8:

\it Pre(fin)\leftarrow P\wedge\neg end

9:

\it Eff(fin)\leftarrow E\cup\{end\}

10:

\it A^{\prime}\leftarrow A\cup\{fin\}

11:

return

\it\langle F^{\prime},O,A^{\prime},I\cup\bigcup\limits_{\mathclap{\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{C}}}\{hold_{c}\},G\wedge\bigwedge\limits_{\mathclap{\begin{subarray}{c}c:\mathsf{ST}(\phi)\in\mathcal{C}\ \vee\\
c:\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)\in\mathcal{C}\end{subarray}}}hold_{c}\wedge end,\emptyset\rangle

12:

function

CompileC
(
\it\mathcal{C}
)

13:

\it P,E\leftarrow\{\},\{\}

14:

for all

\it c\in\mathcal{C}

do

15:

if

\it c
is
\it\mathsf{A}(\phi)

then

\it P\leftarrow P\cup\{\phi\}

16:

else

if

\it c
is
\it\mathsf{ST}(\phi)

then

\it E\leftarrow E\cup\{\phi\triangleright hold_{c}\}

17:

else

if

\it c
is
\it\mathsf{AO}(\phi)

then

18:

\it E\leftarrow E\cup\{\phi\triangleright seen_{\phi}\}

19:

\it E\leftarrow E\cup\{(\neg\phi\wedge seen_{\phi})\triangleright prevent_{\phi}\}

20:

\it P\leftarrow P\cup\{\neg(\phi\wedge prevent_{\phi})\}

21:

else

if

\it c
is
\it\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)

then

22:

\it E\leftarrow E\cup\{\psi\triangleright seen_{\psi}\}

23:

\it P\leftarrow P\cup\{\phi\rightarrow seen_{\psi}\}

24:

else

if

\it c
is
\it\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)

then

25:

\it E\leftarrow E\cup\{(\phi\wedge\neg\psi)\triangleright\neg hold_{c}\}\cup\{\psi\triangleright hold_{c}\}

26:

return

\it P
,
\it E

Algorithm
2
outlines the steps of LCC. Initially, the compilation creates the necessary monitoring atoms to track the status of the constraints (line
3
).
LCC requires the same monitoring atoms used in LiftedTCORE, plus an additional atom
\it prevent_{\phi}
for every
\it\mathsf{AO}(\phi)
.
This atom express that
\it\phi
is false after having been true, and thus should be prevented from becoming true again in order to satisfy
\it\mathsf{AO}(\phi)
.

Next, LCC determines the set of preconditions
\it P
and the set of effects
\it E
to be added to every action (line
4
) by iterating over each constraint
\it c
(lines
14
-
25
).
If
\it c
is
\it\mathsf{A}(\phi)
, LCC adds precondition
\it\phi
in all actions, ensuring that no progress can be made when we have
\it\neg\phi
(line
15
).
If
\it c
is
\it\mathsf{ST}(\phi)
, LCC captures the satisfaction of
\it c
by making
\it hold_{c}
true when
\it\phi
holds (line
16
).
If
\it c
is
\it\mathsf{AO}(\phi)
, LCC brings about
\it seen_{\phi}
when
\it\phi
is true (line
18
), and
\it prevent_{\phi}
when
\it\phi
is false after having been true in the past (line
19
).
Then, LCC prevents the execution of any action when both
\it\phi
and
\it prevent_{\phi}
are true, a situation that violates
\it\mathsf{AO}(\phi)
(line
20
).
If
\it c
is
\it\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)
, LCC sets
\it seen_{\psi}
when
\it\psi
becomes true, and prevents further actions when
\it\phi
is true and
\it seen_{\psi}
is false (lines
22
-
23
).
Lastly, if
\it c
is
\it\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)
, LCC activates
\it hold_{c}
when
\it\psi
is true, and deactivates it when
\it\phi
is true and
\it\psi
is false (line
25
), thus expressing that
\it\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)
is violated only when
\it\phi
held at some point in the past and
\it\psi
has not become true since then.

Preconditions
\it P
and effects
\it E
are added to every action of the problem, including the newly introduced
\it fin
action (lines
5
-
9
).
To ensure that no further action is executable after
\it fin
, we add precondition
\it\neg end
to every action, and
\it end
as an effect of
\it fin
.
As a last step, LCC determines the new goal, i.e.,
\it G
augmented with the conjunction of all
\it hold_{c}
atoms and atom
\it end
, and the new initial state, where every
\it hold_{c}
atom for a
\it\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)
constraint is set to true, maintaining correctness in the case where both
\it\phi
and
\it\psi
are always false.

Example 7

(LCC Compiler)
.

LCC compiles the problem in Example
6
using the monitoring atoms
\it hold_{\mathsf{ST}(\mathtt{clear}(b_{5}))}
,
\it seen_{\psi}
,
\it seen_{\phi}
and
\it prevent_{\phi}
, where
\it\psi
is
\it\exists topb:\mathtt{on}(topb,b_{3})
and
\it\phi
is
\it\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1})
, and then extends all actions with the following preconditions
\it P
and effects
\it E
:

\it\displaystyle P\val\{

\it\displaystyle\mathtt{clear}(b_{5})\rightarrow seen_{\psi},\neg(\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1})\wedge prevent_{\phi})\}

\it\displaystyle E\val\{

\it\displaystyle\mathtt{clear}(b_{5})\triangleright hold_{c_{1}},\exists topb:\mathtt{on}(topb,b_{3})\triangleright seen_{\psi},

\it\displaystyle\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1})\triangleright seen_{\phi},

\it\displaystyle(\neg\mathtt{onTable}(b_{1})\wedge seen_{\phi})\triangleright prevent_{\phi}\}

\it\blacksquare

Proposition 3

(Correctness of LCC)
.

If LCC compiles a problem
\it\Pi
into problem
\it\Pi^{\prime}
, then plan
\it\langle a^{\mathbin{\text{\hbox to4.6pt{\vbox to4.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.30002pt\lower-2.03357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}
{}{}
{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.34 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{
{}{}}}{
{}{}}
{{}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}
{}
{}
{}
{}
{}
{}
{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.91 0.37 L 2.91 0.37 M -1.94 -1.08 L 1.94 -1.08 M -0.97 -2.54 L 0.97 -2.54}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces

\it\blacklozenge

Proposition 4

(Complexity of LCC)
.

Suppose that
\it n_{a}
and
\it n_{c}
denote, respectively, the number of actions and the number of constraint in some problem
\it\Pi
.
The worst-case time complexity of compiling
\it\Pi
with LCC is
\it\mathcal{O}(n_{a}{+}n_{c})
.

\it\blacklozenge

5
Comparing LiftedTCORE and LCC

We highlight the main differences between our compilers.

Introduced Preconditions and Effects.

Through the use of regression, LiftedTCORE identifies action-specific preconditions and effects that capture the constraints, while LCC adds the required preconditions and effects in all actions in a uniform way.
This action-specific compilation of LiftedTCORE allows us to avoid redundant updates to actions that are irrelevant to a constraint, leading to succinct compiled problems.
In contrast, the action-agnostic compilation of LCC does not permit such optimisations.

Constraint Evaluation Delay.
LCC monitors constraints with a 1-step delay, i.e., the status of constraint in a state
\it s
can only be identified after an action
\it a
is applied in
\it s
.
On the other hand, LiftedTCORE does not delay the evaluation of constraints, in the sense that the status of a constraint in state
\it s
was calculated at the time of reaching
\it s
through the evaluation of regression formulae.

Monitoring Atoms.
LCC uses the same monitoring atoms as LiftedTCORE, plus one
\it prevent_{\phi}
atom for each
\it\mathsf{AO}(\phi)
. Unlike LiftedTCORE, LCC cannot foresee when an action will cause
\it\phi
to hold for a second time, and thus uses
\it prevent_{\phi}
to block further actions once this occurs.

Initial State.
In LiftedTCORE, to express the status of the constraints in the initial state, we need to add to that state the monitoring atoms that hold initially.
This is not required in LCC because of its delayed constraint evaluation, i.e., the status of a constraint in the initial state is expressed by the truth values of the monitoring atoms in its subsequent state.

Actions.
LCC requires 1 additional action compared to LiftedTCORE, i.e., action
\it fin
, which is required to check whether the constraints are satisfied in a goal state, due to the 1-step constraint evaluation delay in LCC.

Complexity.
While both LiftedTCORE and LCC operate in polynomial time to the size of the problem (see Propositions
2
and
4
), LCC runs in time that is
linear
in the number of constraints and the number of actions, as because it determines all preconditions and effects required for the compilation with one pass over the constraints.
Instead, LiftedTCORE derives a set of preconditions and effects for each action of the domain, which more computationally involved.

Our aim is to evaluate LiftedTCORE and LCC against state-of-the-art approaches.
To do this, we generated a new benchmark based on the
\it 7
domains of the latest International Planning Competition (IPC)
(
35
)
, including problems with many objects and high-arity actions that are challenging for grounding-based compilations.
Specifically, we created two datasets, each with
\it 20
tasks per domain: one with ground constraints, denoted by “Ground”, and one with constraints containing quantifiers (
\it\forall
,
\it\exists
), denoted by “Non-Ground”.
These datasets contained
\it 280
problems in total and were generated as follows.
First, we used the IPC generators to construct problem instances without constraints.
Second, we introduced ground constraints that complicate an optimal solution of the problem using a custom constraint generator, yielding the Ground dataset.
Third, we modified the produced ground constraints by introducing quantifiers, leading to our Non-Ground dataset.
Additional details on constraint generation, along with statistics on the generated constraints, are provided in Appendix
D
.

We used TCORE as our baseline, as it has been proven more effective
(
4
)
than other methods
(
11
;
3
;
20
;
36
)
for handling PDDL constraints. We also included LTL-C, a lifted compiler that is specifically designed for quantified constraints
(
2
)
2
2

2

We provide further discussion on LTL-C in Appendix
C.1
.
.
All compiled instances were solved using LAMA
(
28
)
, a state-of-the-art satisficing planner.
We configured LAMA to stop after finding the first solution and disabled its invariant generation, as this allowed all evaluated systems to solve more instances. All experiments were run on an Intel Xeon Gold 6140M 2.3 GHz, with runtime and memory limits of 1800s and 8GB, respectively.
LiftedTCORE
3

, LCC
4

and our benchmark

are publicly available.

6.1
Experimental Results

Table
1
presents the coverage (number of solved problems) per domain achieved by LAMA when planning over the compiled specification of each system,
We observe that TCORE, LiftedTCORE, and LCC performed comparably. Overall, LCC achieved the best performance, with TCORE being a close runner-up—these IPC domains were designed to be compatible with traditional grounding-based planners like LAMA, and thus this grounding-based planner performed well on tasks compiled by TCORE. Nonetheless, both LCC and LiftedTCORE were competitive with TCORE and outperformed LTL-C, the state of the art among lifted compilation approaches. LTL-C failed in the ‘Recharging’ and ‘Rubik’s’ domains, as it does not support universally quantified effects, which are present in these domains.

Figure 1:
Runtime comparison between LiftedTCORE vs. TCORE and between LiftedTCORE vs. LCC.

In terms of coverage, LCC outperformed LiftedTCORE; LiftedTCORE’s lifted regression operator introduced complex precondition and effect formulas, especially in cases with Non-Ground constraints, hindering LAMA’s performance.
On the other hand, this operator allows constraint evaluation without the 1-step delay of LCC, allowing LAMA to perform a more efficient search. Table
2
reports the average number of nodes expanded by LAMA when planning over the compiled specifications of LCC, LiftedTCORE and TCORE. On average, LiftedTCORE led to fewer node expansions than LCC. TCORE and LiftedTCORE led to a similar number of node expansions, as they both employ regression to foresee redundant expansions.

The reduction in expansion nodes also affects runtime; Figure
1
shows that LiftedTCORE led to more efficient planning than LCC in several instances.
Specifically, constraint compilation with LiftedTCORE led to more efficient planning than LCC compilation in 161 out of the 234 instances that were solved by both approaches.
LiftedTCORE performed better on instances with Ground constraints, where both approaches introduces formulas with comparable complexity.
In the Non-Ground case, the size of the largest formula introduced by LiftedTCORE and LCC per instance was, on average, 26 and 18 atoms, thus leading to slower planning in the case of LiftedTCORE.
Our results show that LiftedTCORE compilation led to slower planning than TCORE compilation, although there is complementarity.

We conclude with statistics on the size of the compiled problems and their compilation times, reported in Table
3
. On average, TCORE produced problems with thousands of actions and effects, reaching up to 411K actions and 1.8M effects, while both LiftedTCORE and LCC produced problems that are more succinct by orders of magnitude. This not only makes the compiled problems more understandable and easier to debug, but also facilitates the use of planners that do not need to perform grounding. Currently, the support of complex preconditions and conditional effects is limited in these planners
(
16
;
18
;
9
)
. We expect that the benefits of our lifted compilers will become more prominent as more expressive heuristics for lifted planning are developed.

Limitations:
We observed that lifted constraint compilers may not be beneficial for problems with a large number of constraints. Lifted methods update all (lifted) actions that may affect the corresponding constraints in a least one of their instantiations, resulting in numerous new action preconditions and effects that hinder the performance of modern planners like LAMA.
In contrast, TCORE updates ground actions sparsely, as only few of them may affect each constraint.
We evaluated our compilers on a benchmark including problems with hundreds of constraints
(
4
)
, and TCORE proved to be the best performing compiler (see Appendix
E
).
We believe that further research on lifted planning heuristics could alleviate this issue, while preserving the benefits of lifted compilations, such as compilation efficiency and compiled specification succinctness.

7
Summary and Further Work

We proposed two methods, LiftedTCORE and LCC, for compiling away quantitative state-trajectory constraints from a planning problem without grounding it.
We studied both compilers theoretically, proving their correctness and deducing their worst-case time complexity, and qualitatively compared their key features.
Moreover, we presented an empirical evaluation of our methods on the domains included in the latest International Planning Competition, demonstrating that our compilers are efficient and lead to significantly more succinct compiled specifications compared to a state-of-the-art compiler that grounds the domain, while yielding competitive performance when used for planning with LAMA.

In the future, we would like to design lifted compilation methods for numeric and metric time PDDL constraints
(
6
)
.

\it\alpha
), which relates the conditions under which actions
\it a
and
\it a^{\mathbin{\text{\hbox to4.6pt{\vbox to4.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.30002pt\lower-2.03357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

may bring about
\it f|_{\theta_{f}}
.
We prove both directions by contradiction.
We use the fact that, for each effect
\it\forall\vec{z}{:}c\triangleright e
of
\it a
,
\it a^{\mathbin{\text{\hbox to4.6pt{\vbox to4.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.30002pt\lower-2.03357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

By combining the results of the two cases, we prove equivalence (
1
) when
\it\psi
is an atom
\it f

For the inductive step, we assume that equivalence (
1
) holds for first-order formulae
\it\phi
,
\it\phi_{1}
and
\it\phi_{2}
, and prove that equivalence (
1
) holds for formulae
\it\neg\phi
,
\it\phi_{1}\wedge\phi_{2}
,
\it\phi_{1}\vee\phi_{2}
,
\it\forall\vec{x}\phi
and
\it\exists\vec{x}\phi
.
We leverage the following properties of the regression operator, which hold because operator
\it R^{L}
only induces changes in
\it\phi
at the atom level, while preserving the structure of
\it\phi
(see Definition
6
).

\it\displaystyle R^{L}(\neg\phi,a)

\it\displaystyle\iff\neg R^{L}(\phi,a)

\it\displaystyle R^{L}(\phi_{1}\wedge\phi_{2},a)

\it\displaystyle\iff R^{L}(\phi_{1},a)\wedge R^{L}(\phi_{2},a)

\it\displaystyle R^{L}(\phi_{1}\vee\phi_{2},a)

\it\displaystyle\iff R^{L}(\phi_{1},a)\vee R^{L}(\phi_{2},a)

\it\displaystyle R^{L}(\forall\vec{x}\phi,a)

\it\displaystyle\iff\forall\vec{x}R^{L}(\phi,a)

\it\displaystyle R^{L}(\exists\vec{x}\phi,a)

\it\displaystyle\iff\exists\vec{x}R^{L}(\phi,a)

Case
\it\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)
.
\it p_{c}
is
\it R^{L}(\phi,a)|_{\theta_{i}}\rightarrow seen_{\psi}
, and thus
\it s^{\prime}_{i}\models R^{L}(\phi,a)|_{\theta_{i}}\wedge\neg seen_{\psi}
. The monitoring atom
\it seen_{\psi}
is true iff there exists a state
\it s^{\prime}_{j}
,
\it j\leq i
, where
\it\psi
is true (see line
23
of Algorithm
1
).
Since
\it\phi
and
\it\psi
do not contain monitoring atoms, we have
\it s_{i}\models R^{L}(\phi,a)|_{\theta_{i}}
and that there is no state
\it s_{j}
,
\it j\leq i
, where
\it\psi
is true.
Thus,
\it\phi
holds in state
\it s_{i}[a^{\mathbin{\text{\hbox to4.6pt{\vbox to4.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.30002pt\lower-2.03357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}
{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{}}{{}}{}

{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.34 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-0.4}{0.3}{0.0}{0.0pt}{-0.7836pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{}}_{i}]
and there is no earlier state where
\it\psi
holds, which is a contradiction because it violates constraint
\it\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)
.

Case 2 expresses that the final state
\it s^{\prime}_{n}
induced by plan
\it\pi
on problem
\it\Pi^{\prime}
does not satisfy its goal
\it G^{\prime}
.
Based on Algorithm
1
,
\it G^{\prime}
is strictly more restrictive that goal
\it G
, as it additionally requires that all the
\it hold_{c}
monitoring atoms are true.
Moreover, we have
\it s_{n}\models G
, because
\it\pi
is a solution for
\it\Pi
, which implies that
\it s^{\prime}_{n}\models G
, as
\it G
does not contain monitoring atoms.
As a result,
\it s^{\prime}_{n}
must be violating a
\it hold_{c}
atom.
Thus, there is a constraint
\it c\val\mathsf{ST}(\phi)
or
\it c\val\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)
in
\it\Pi
such that
\it s^{\prime}_{n}\not\models hold_{c}
.

•

Case
\it c
is
\it\mathsf{ST}(\phi)
. Since
\it hold_{c}
may only come about via effect
\it R^{L}(\phi,a)\triangleright hold_{c}
and

Case
\it\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)
. There is a state
\it s_{j}
where
\it\phi\wedge\neg\psi
holds and there is no state after
\it s_{i}
,
\it i>j
, where
\it\psi
holds.
This implies that
\it s^{\prime}_{j}\models\phi\wedge\neg\psi
and
\it\forall j<i\leq n:s^{\prime}_{i}\models\neg\psi
.
Then, based on the effects in lines
25
–
26
of Algorithm
1
,
\it hold_{c}
does not hold in the final state
\it s^{\prime}_{n}
, which is a contradiction because
\it hold_{c}
is required by goal
\it G^{\prime}
.

By contradiction, we have shown that if plan
\it\pi
is a solution for
\it\Pi^{\prime}
then it is also a solution for
\it\Pi
.

Therefore, if LiftedTCORE compiles a problem
\it\Pi
into problem
\it\Pi^{\prime}
, then a plan
\it\pi
is valid for
\it\Pi
iff
\it\pi
is valid for
\it\Pi^{\prime}
.
∎

A.3
Proof of Proposition
2

See
2

Proof.

The first step of LiftedTCORE is to check whether a constraint is violated in the initial state of the problem, rendering it unsolvable (see lines
3
–
4
of Algorithm
1
).
To do this, LiftedTCORE iterates over the constraints in
\it C
and, in the worst-case, solves a model checking
\it I\models\phi
problem for each constraint.
The cost of this model checking problem is
\it\mathcal{O}(n_{f}^{b})

(
37
)
, and thus the cost of this step is
\it\mathcal{O}(n_{c}n_{f}^{b})
.
Second, LiftedTCORE introduces the necessary monitoring atoms for the constraints in
\it C
, which is done in
\it n_{c}
steps (line
5
).
Third, LiftedTCORE identifies the monitoring atoms that need to be added in the initial state (line
6
).
The cost of identifying these atoms is
\it\mathcal{O}(n_{c}n_{f}^{b})
, as it requires solving model checking problems, similarly to the first step.
Fourth, LiftedTCORE iterates over each action-constraint pair in
\it\Pi
and computes the preconditions and effects that need to be added to the action (lines
8
–
11
and lines
14
–
28
).
Identifying these preconditions and effects requires, in the worst case, the evaluation of two lifted regression expressions.
To evaluate
\it R^{L}(\phi,a)
for a formula
\it\phi
and action
\it a
, we need to compute
\it R^{L}(f,a)
for each atom
\it f
in
\it\phi
, whose number is bounded by
\it n_{f}
.

\it R^{L}(f,a)
requires the computation of
\it\Gamma^{L}_{f}(a)
and
\it\Gamma^{L}_{\neg f}(a)
.
To derive
\it\Gamma^{L}_{l}(a)
, we need to compute, for each effect
\it e
of action
\it a
—whose number is bounded by
\it n_{f}
—the most general unifier
\it\xi(l,e)
, which requires
\it n_{k}
steps, i.e., one iteration over the argument of
\it l
and
\it e
.
Therefore, the overall cost of computing the preconditions and effects that need to be added to the actions of the problem is
\it\mathcal{O}(n_{a}n_{c}n_{f}^{2}n_{k})
.
Fifth, LiftedTCORE added to the goal state all
\it hold_{c}
monitoring atoms, which requires at most
\it n_{c}
steps (line
12
).

Based on the above steps, the worst-case time complexity of LiftedTCORE is
\it\mathcal{O}(2n_{c}n_{f}^{b}{+}2n_{c}{+}n_{a}n_{c}n^{2}_{f}n_{k})
, which, after simplifications, becomes
\it\mathcal{O}(n_{c}n_{f}^{b}{+}n_{a}n_{c}n^{2}_{f}n_{k})
.
∎

Appendix B
Proofs for Section
4

B.1
Proof of Proposition
3

See
3

Proof.

Let:

•

\it\Pi\val\langle F,O,A,I,G,C\rangle

•

\it\Pi^{\prime}\val\langle F^{\prime},O,A^{\prime},I^{\prime},G^{\prime},\emptyset\rangle

•

Additionally,
\it\sigma\val\langle s_{0},\dots,s_{n}\rangle
and
\it\sigma^{\prime}\val\langle s^{\prime}_{0},\dots,s^{\prime}_{n},s^{\prime}_{f}\rangle
denote the state trajectories induced by executing plans
\it\pi
and
\it\pi^{\prime}
on problems
\it\Pi
and
\it\Pi^{\prime}
, respectively, while
\it Pre^{\prime}(a)
and
\it Eff^{\prime}(a)
denote the preconditions and the effects of an action
\it a
in the compiled problem
\it\Pi^{\prime}
.

\it s^{\prime}_{i}\models Pre(a^{\mathbin{\text{\hbox to4.6pt{\vbox to4.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.30002pt\lower-2.03357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

.
Moreover, because
\it Pre^{\prime}(a^{\mathbin{\text{\hbox to4.6pt{\vbox to4.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.30002pt\lower-2.03357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

and
\it s^{\prime}_{i}\not\models Pre^{\prime}(a^{\mathbin{\text{\hbox to4.6pt{\vbox to4.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.30002pt\lower-2.03357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

{{}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M 0 3.34 L 0 0}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{

{{}\lxSVG@stroke\lxSVG@drawpath@unclipped{M -2.91 0.37 L 2.91 0.37 M -1.94 -1.08 L 1.94 -1.08 M -0.97 -2.54 L 0.97 -2.54}{fill:none} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces
}{{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@transformcm{0.0}{-0.4}{0.3}{0.0}{0.0pt}{-0.7836pt}\lxSVG@begingroup@{transform} \pgfsys@hbox{60}\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope }}}
\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{}}_{i})
, there is some
\it p_{c}\in\{p_{1},\dots,p_{k}\}
such that
\it s^{\prime}_{i}\not\models p_{c}
.
Based on Algorithm
2
, precondition
\it p_{c}
is introduced by the compilation of either an
\it\mathsf{A}(\phi)
, an
\it\mathsf{AO}(\phi)
or a
\it\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)
constraints.

•

Case
\it\mathsf{A}(\phi)
.
\it p_{c}
is
\it\phi
, and thus
\it s^{\prime}_{i}\not\models\phi
, which, since
\it\phi
does not contain monitoring atoms, implies that
\it s_{i}\not\models\phi
. This is a contradiction because
\it s_{i}
is a state induced by executing solution
\it\pi
of
\it\Pi
and
\it\Pi
has constraint
\it\mathsf{A}(\phi)
.

•

Case
\it\mathsf{AO}(\phi)
.
\it p_{c}
is
\it\neg(\phi\wedge prevent_{\phi})
, and thus
\it s^{\prime}_{i}\models\phi\wedge prevent_{\phi}
. According to the effects of the actions in
\it A^{\prime}
, the monitoring atom
\it prevent_{\phi}
is true iff there is a state
\it s^{\prime}_{j}
,
\it j<i
, where
\it\phi
is false and atom
\it seen_{\phi}
is true (see line
18
of Algorithm
2
). In turn,
\it seen_{\phi}
is true iff there is a state
\it s^{\prime}_{l}
,
\it l<j
, where
\it\phi
is true (line
19
).
Since
\it\phi
does not contain monitoring atoms, the above implies that the states
\it s_{l}
,
\it s_{j}
and
\it s_{i}
of solution
\it\pi
of
\it\Pi
are such that
\it\phi
is true in
\it s_{l}
,
\it\phi
is false in
\it s_{j}
and
\it\phi
is true in
\it s_{i}
.
This violates constraint
\it\mathsf{AO}(\phi)
of
\it\Pi
leading to a contradiction.

•

Case
\it\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)
.
\it p_{c}
is
\it\phi\rightarrow seen_{\psi}
, and thus
\it s^{\prime}_{i}\models\phi\wedge\neg seen_{\psi}
. The monitoring atom
\it seen_{\psi}
is true iff there exists a state
\it s^{\prime}_{j}
,
\it j<i
, where
\it\psi
is true (line
22
).
Since
\it\phi
and
\it\psi
do not contain monitoring atoms, we have
\it s_{i}\models\phi
and that there is no state
\it s_{j}
,
\it j<i
, preceding
\it s_{i}
where
\it\psi
is true.
This is a contradiction because
\it\pi
is a solution of
\it\Pi
and
\it\Pi
includes constraint
\it\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)
.

Case 2 expresses that state
\it s^{\prime}_{n}
does not satisfy the precondition of the
\it fin
of problem
\it\Pi^{\prime}
, which is the conjunction of the preconditions
\it p_{1},\dots,p_{k}
discovered during constraint compilation.
We work as in case 1; we assume that
\it s^{\prime}_{n}\not\models p_{i}
,
\it 1\leq i\leq k
, and discover that this implies that a constraint of problem
\it\Pi
is violated at state
\it s_{n}
, contradicting the assumption that
\it\pi
is a solution for
\it\Pi
.

Case 3 expresses that the final state
\it s^{\prime}_{f}
of plan
\it\pi^{\prime}
does not satisfy the goal
\it G^{\prime}
of problem
\it\Pi^{\prime}
.
Based on Algorithm
2
,
\it G^{\prime}
is strictly more restrictive that goal
\it G
, as it additionally requires that all the
\it hold_{c}
monitoring atoms and the
\it end
atom are true.
The
\it end
atom is true in
\it s^{\prime}_{f}
because it is an effect of action
\it fin
.
Moreover, we have
\it s_{n}\models G
, because
\it\pi
is a solution for
\it\Pi
, which implies that
\it s^{\prime}_{n}\models G
, as
\it G
does not contain monitoring atoms.
In turn
\it s^{\prime}_{n}\models G
implies that
\it s^{\prime}_{f}\models G
because the
\it fin
does not affect the atoms in
\it G
.
As a result,
\it s^{\prime}_{f}
must be violating a
\it hold_{c}
atom.
Thus, there is a constraint
\it c\val\mathsf{ST}(\phi)
or
\it c\val\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)
in
\it\Pi
such that
\it s^{\prime}_{f}\not\models hold_{c}
.

•

Case
\it\mathsf{ST}(\phi)
. Since every action in
\it A^{\prime}
has effect
\it\phi\triangleright hold_{c}
and
\it s^{\prime}_{f}\not\models hold_{c}
, then there is no state in
\it\sigma^{\prime}
where
\it\phi
holds.
This implies that neither
\it\sigma
contains such a state, which is a contradiction because
\it\pi
solves
\it\Pi
and
\it\mathsf{ST}(\phi)
is one of its constraints.

•

Case
\it\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)
. Based on the effects manipulating
\it hold_{c}
and the fact that
\it hold_{c}
is true initially, we
\it s^{\prime}_{f}\not\models hold_{c}
iff there is a state where
\it\phi\wedge\neg\psi
holds and it is not followed by a state where
\it\psi
holds.
Moving this situation to the trace
\it\sigma
induced by plan
\it\pi
, we deduce that this plan violates constraint
\it\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)
of
\it\Pi
, which is a contradiction because
\it\pi
is a solution of
\it\Pi
.

By contradicting cases 1–3, we have shown that if
\it\pi
is solution for
\it\Pi
then plan
\it\pi^{\prime}
is a solution for
\it\Pi^{\prime}
.

Suppose that
\it\pi^{\prime}
is a solution for
\it\Pi^{\prime}
, but
\it\pi
is not solution for
\it\Pi
.
One of the following must hold:

1.

Case 2 cannot be true because we have
\it s^{\prime}_{f}\models G^{\prime}
, which is implies
\it s_{n}\models G
.

We treat case 3 depending on the type of constraint
\it c
.

Case
\it\mathsf{A}(\phi)
. There is a state
\it s_{i}
such that
\it s_{i}\models\neg\phi
, which implies that
\it s^{\prime}_{i}\models\neg\phi
.

\it Pre^{\prime}(a^{\mathbin{\text{\hbox to4.6pt{\vbox to4.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 2.30002pt\lower-2.03357pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}

\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}}}{}}_{i})
requires that
\it\phi
holds, that makes plan
\it\pi^{\prime}
not executable, which is a contradiction.

•

Case
\it\mathsf{ST}(\phi)
. There is no state in
\it\sigma
where
\it\phi
holds. As a result, there is neither such a state in
\it\sigma^{\prime}
, meaning that effect
\it\phi\triangleright hold_{c}
is never triggered.
Thus,
\it hold_{c}
does not hold at
\it s^{\prime}_{f}
, which is a contradiction as
\it hold_{c}
is required in the goal of
\it\Pi^{\prime}
.

•

Case
\it\mathsf{AO}(\phi)
. There are states
\it s_{l}
,
\it s_{j}
and
\it s_{i}
such that
\it l<j<i
,
\it\phi
is true in
\it s_{l}
,
\it\phi
is false in
\it s_{j}
and
\it\phi
is true in
\it s_{i}
.
This implies that
\it\phi
has the corresponding truth values in states
\it s^{\prime}_{l}
,
\it s^{\prime}_{j}
and
\it s^{\prime}_{i}
.
Based on the action effects in
\it\Pi^{\prime}
,
\it seen_{\phi}
holds after state
\it s^{\prime}_{l}
and
\it prevent_{\phi}
holds after state
\it s^{\prime}_{j}
.
As a result, both
\it\phi
and
\it prevent_{\phi}
hold in state
\it s^{\prime}_{i}
, making action
\it a_{i}
inapplicable, which is a contradiction.

•

Case
\it\mathsf{SB}(\phi,\psi)
. There is a state
\it s_{i}
where
\it\phi
holds and there is no state
\it s_{j}
,
\it j<i
, where
\it\psi
holds.
This implies that
\it s^{\prime}_{i}\models\phi
and
\it\forall j<i:s^{\prime}_{j}\models\neg\psi
.
As a result, action effect
\it\psi\triangleright seen_{\psi}
is never satisfied before
\it s^{\prime}_{i}
, meaning that
\it seen_{\psi}
is false in
\it s^{\prime}_{i}
.
Therefore, based on precondition
\it\phi\rightarrow seen_{\psi}
, action
\it a_{i}
is not applicable in state
\it s^{\prime}_{i}
, which is a contradiction.

•

Case
\it\mathsf{SA}(\phi,\psi)
. There is a state
\it s_{j}
where
\it\phi\wedge\neg\psi
holds and there is no state after
\it s_{i}
,
\it i>j
, where
\it\psi
holds.
This implies that
\it s^{\prime}_{j}\models\phi\wedge\neg\psi
and
\it\forall j<i\leq n\vee i=f:s^{\prime}_{i}\models\neg\psi
.
Therefore, based on action effects
\it\{\phi\wedge\neg\psi\triangleright\neg hold_{c}\}
and
\it\{\psi\triangleright hold_{c}\}
,
\it hold_{c}
does not hold in the final state
\it s^{\prime}_{f}
, which is a contradiction because
\it hold_{c}
is required by goal
\it G^{\prime}
.

By contradiction, we have shown that if plan
\it\pi^{\prime}
is a solution for
\it\Pi^{\prime}
then
\it\pi
is solution for
\it\Pi
.

B.2
Proof of Proposition
4

See
4

Proof.

In order to compile away the constraints
\it C
in problem
\it\Pi
, LCC first needs to update the set of atoms
\it F
of
\it\Pi
with monitoring atoms (see line
3
of Algorithm
2
).
This is achieved in one pass over the constraints in
\it C
, and thus the cost of this operation is
\it\mathcal{O}(n_{c})
.
Second, LCC iterates once over each constraint
\it c\in C
in order to identify the set of preconditions
\it P
and the set of effects
\it E
that need to be added to the actions of
\it\Pi
(lines
14
–
25
).
In each iteration, LCC adds a fixed number of preconditions and effects in sets
\it P
and
\it E
, and thus the cost of each iteration is
\it\mathcal{O}(1)
.
Therefore, the cost of computing sets
\it P
and
\it E
is
\it\mathcal{O}(n_{c})
.
Third, LCC adds preconditions
\it P
and effects
\it E
to every action in
\it\Pi
, as well as to action
\it fin
, which requires
\it n_{a}{+}1
operations (lines
5
–
10
).
Fourth, LCC updates the initial state and the goal with
\it hold_{c}
atoms by performing one iteration over the constraints in
\it C
(line
11
).

Based on the above steps, the worst-case time complexity of LCC is
\it\mathcal{O}(3n_{c}{+}n_{a}{+}1)
, which, after simplifications, becomes
\it\mathcal{O}(n_{a}{+}n_{c})
.
∎

Appendix D
Datasets

D.1
Constrained Planning Problem Generator

Figure 2:
Constrained planning problem generator.

We used a custom generator to introduce constraints to planning problems from the domains used in the latest International Planning Competition (IPC).
Figure
2
presents the architecture of our constrained planning problem generator.
We provide as input to our generator a PDDL domain file and a problem instance generator for one of the planning domains used in our analysis, both of which have been made public by the latest IPC.
The output of our generator is a PDDL planning problem with constraints for the provided domain.

Our generator operates as follows.
First, we sample a problem file using the input problem generator (see ‘Sampler’ in Figure
2
); this problem file does not include constraints.
Second, we invoke the off-the-shelf planner
SymK
to derive an optimal plan for the sampled problem.
Third, we generate constraints for the sampled problem (see ‘Constraint Generator’).
Constraint generation is task-aware, in the sense that we guide the search towards constraints that complicate the optimal solution of the unconstrained problem discovered by
SymK
in the previous step, while preserving feasibility, i.e., the constrained problem has a solution.
We add the produced constraints to the sampled problem file, leading to planning problem with constraints.
Fourth, we use LiftedTCORE to compile away the constraints from this problem and, fifth, we invoke
SymK
to identify an optimal plan for the constrained planning problem.
If this plan is longer than the optimal plan of the original (unconstrained) problem, then we add the generated constrained planning problem to the dataset of our experimental evaluation.

Figure 3:
Initial state of a problem from the ‘Ricochet Robots’ domain. ‘R1’, ‘R2’, ‘R3’ and ‘R4’ denote robots, ‘G3’ is a goal location, and ‘X’ symbols denote walls.

In order to construct constraints with quantifiers, we instruct our generator to modify the constraints it produced by replacing a subset of their constants with variables and introducing quantifiers referring to the newly added variables.

We illustrate the constraint generation step of our generator with the following example.

Example 8
.

Consider the ‘Ricochet Robots’ planning domain, which comprises a 2D gridworld with walls blocking movement between certain squares.
A square on the board may be empty, contain a robot or a goal location.
The task is to move each robot to its designated goal location.
Robots can be moved by pushing them towards a cardinal direction; after being pushed, a robot continues moving until it reaches a wall or another robot.

Figure
3
depicts the initial state of a problem from the ‘Ricochet Robots’ domain.
The goal is to bring robot ‘R3’ in goal location ‘G3’.
The optimal plan to solve this problem consists of two ‘push’ actions.
The first one pushes robot R3 east, making it move one step until it hits robot R1.
The second push action moves R3 north, making it stop at goal location G3, as it hits the northern border of the gridworld.

Our constraint generator analyses the trajectory induced by the above optimal plan of the problem and identifies a set of atoms whose inclusion in a constraint may complicate this planning problem.
In our example, executing the optimal plan yields a state where robot R3 is in location ‘L23’, i.e., the square below G3.
Our constraint generator recognises that, if we were to disallow robot R3 from stepping on square L23, then the previously discovered optimal plan would not be executable, necessitating a lengthier plan to solve the problem.
Thus, our constraint generator may produce constraint
\it\mathsf{A}(\neg(\mathtt{at}(\mathtt{R3},\mathtt{L23})))
, stating that robot R3 must never be on square L23.
As a result of adding this constraint to the problem, the length of its optimal solution becomes 4 moves: (i) moving R3 west, (ii) moving R3 north, (iii) moving R1 north and (iv) moving R3 west, after which R3 hits R1, thus stopping at goal location G3.

Our constraint generator may also complicate the problem by introducing constraints imposing intermediate goals.
For instance, in our example problem, we may generate constraint
\it\mathsf{ST}(\mathtt{at}(\mathtt{R3},\mathtt{L32}))
, stating that, at some point during plan execution, robot R3 needs to be in location L32, i.e., the square that is below the starting position of R3.
Moreover, our generator may modify sampled constraints by introducing quantifiers.
Constraint
\it\mathsf{A}(\neg(\mathtt{at}(\mathtt{R3},\mathtt{L23})))
, e.g., may be transformed as
\it\mathsf{A}(\forall R:\neg(\mathtt{at}(R,\mathtt{L23})))
, expressing that no robot is ever allowed to step on square L23, while constraint
\it\mathsf{ST}(\mathtt{at}(\mathtt{R3},\mathtt{L32}))
may be transformed as
\it\mathsf{ST}(\exists R:\mathtt{at}(R,\mathtt{L32}))
, expressing that, at some point during plan execution, there is a robot that is located on square L32.

D.2
Constraint Type Statistics

We report the constraint type distribution in our dataset.
Tables
4
and
5
present the number of constraints of each type, i.e.,
\it\mathsf{A}
,
\it\mathsf{ST}
,
\it\mathsf{AO}
,
\it\mathsf{SB}
or
\it\mathsf{SA}
, per IPC domain, in our dataset with ground constraints, and in out dataset with non-ground constraints, respectively.
These constraints were produced by our constraints generator (Appendix
D.1
), given domain files and unconstrained problem instances for the IPC domains.
We observe that our generator more often complicated the unconstrained problem by introducing constraints that impose intermediate goals, i.e.,
\it\mathsf{ST}
,
\it\mathsf{SB}
and
\it\mathsf{SA}
.
On the other hand, it was often more difficult to find
\it\mathsf{A}
and
\it\mathsf{AO}
constraints that complicate the problem while maintaining solvability, resulting in relatively fewer
\it\mathsf{A}
and
\it\mathsf{AO}
constraints.

Appendix E
Coverage on ICAPS 2021 Datasets

Table
6
reports the coverage of all evaluated systems on the benchmark introduced by
4
. We excluded the Openstack domain, as it is fully grounded and falls outside the scope of this work. Both LCC and LiftedTCORE perform poorly on this dataset, which contains many constraints that, when compiled, lead to large preconditions and effects. As a result, LAMA often fails during preprocessing, frequently running out of memory on instances compiled by lifted approaches. We were also unable to run experiments on the Storage domain due to incompatibilities with the Unified Planning library used to implement LCC and LiftedTCORE. Among the lifted compilations, LTL-C works well, and this is mainly because it introduces PDDL axioms, which help decompose formulas and reduce the complexity of compiled actions. TCORE performs particularly well on this dataset, as working with the ground representation allows for more targeted, sparse updates to actions.
